# THE SONG CELESTIAL.

<term>The Song Celestial<note>This title is the famous English translation of the *Bhagavad Gita* (literally "The Song of the Lord"). Originally written in Sanskrit, it is a 700-verse scripture that forms a central part of the Great Indian Epic, the *Mahabharata*, and is a cornerstone of Hindu philosophy.</note></term>

<summary>This page serves as the title page for "The Song Celestial," the widely recognized English poetic translation of the Sanskrit Bhagavad Gita.</summary>
<keywords>The Song Celestial, Bhagavad Gita, Hinduism, Sanskrit scripture, title page</keywords>

## NEW WORK BY SIR EDWIN ARNOLD

**THE FIFTY STANZAS OF THE SECRET LOVER:** <note>original Sanskrit: *Chaurapañcāśikā*. This is an 11th-century Sanskrit poem by the poet Bilhana, traditionally said to be written while he was imprisoned for a secret love affair with a princess.</note> Being a Translation of an Ancient Sanskrit Love-Poem. With the Sanskrit Text, and Coloured Illustrations by the Translator, printed in facsimile by Messrs. W. Griggs & Sons, of Peckham. Oblong 8vo, 10s. 6d.

Library Edition, crown 8vo, 6s. ; Lotos Edition, pott 8vo, cloth or half-parchment, 3s. 6d. ; New and dainty edition, small pott 8vo, cloth, 1s. 6d. net ; leather, 2s. net.

**POEMS, NATIONAL AND NON-ORIENTAL,** with some New Pieces. Third Edition. Crown 8vo, 6s.

This is a primary Sanskrit text of Hindu philosophy which features a dialogue between a young boy named Nachiketa and Yama, the god of death.</note> From the Sanskrit. Sixth Edition. Crown 8vo, 6s.

**THE SONG CELESTIAL;** or, **The Song of the Lord.** <note>original: *Bhagavad-Gîtâ*. A 700-verse scripture that is part of the *Mahabharata*, considered one of the most important holy texts in Hinduism.</note> From the Sanskrit. Eighth Edition. Crown 8vo, 5s.

**WITH SA'DI IN THE GARDEN;** or, The Book of Love. Being the “**Divine Love,**” <note>original Persian/Arabic: *Ishk* or *‘Ishq*.</note> or Third Chapter of the “**Orchard**” <note>original Persian: *Bostan*.</note> of the Persian poet “Sa'di,” embodied in a Dialogue. Fourth Edition. Cr. 8vo, 6s.

**POETICAL WORKS.** Uniform Edition, comprising “The Light of Asia,” “Lotus and Jewel,” “Indian Poetry,” “Pearls of the Faith,” “Indian Idylls,” “The Secret of Death,” “The Song Celestial,” “With Sa'di in the Garden.” 8 vols., crown 8vo, £2, 8s.

<meta>This dedication page introduces a translation of the Bhagavad Gita, bridging the original Sanskrit scripture with the English poetic vision of Sir Edwin Arnold.</meta>

# Dedication.

---

->TO INDIA.<-

Thus I have heard this wonderful and hair-stirring dialogue between Krishna <note>original Sanskrit: वासुदेवस्य (Vasudeva)</note> and the noble-minded Arjuna <note>original Sanskrit: पार्थस्य (Partha)</note>. <note>This is a translation of Bhagavad Gita 18.74</note> 
Thus has this wisdom, more secret than the most secret, been declared to you by Me; and among men, there is no one who performs a service more dear to Me than he. <note>This translates Bhagavad Gita 18.63 and 18.69, emphasizing the sharing of spiritual knowledge.</note>

---

*So have I read this wonderful and spirit-thrilling speech,*
*By Krishna and Prince Arjun held, discoursing each with each;*
*So have I writ its wisdom here,—its hidden mystery,*
*For England; O our India! as dear to me as She!*

->EDWIN ARNOLD.<-

<summary>This dedication page features traditional Sanskrit verses from the Bhagavad Gita followed by Sir Edwin Arnold's poetic English interpretation, expressing his devotion to both India and England.</summary>
<keywords>Dedication, Bhagavad Gita, Krishna, Arjuna, Edwin Arnold, Sanskrit translation, India, spiritual wisdom</keywords>

# PREFACE.

---

THIS famous and marvellous Sanskrit poem occurs as an episode of the *Mahâbhârata* <term>Mahabharata</term> <note>One of the two major Sanskrit epics of ancient India, it is a massive narrative detailing the struggle between the Pandava and Kaurava princes.</note>, in the sixth—or “*Bhishma*”—Parva <note>The "Book of Bhishma," the sixth of eighteen major sections of the epic.</note> of the great Hindoo epic. It enjoys immense popularity and authority in India, where it is reckoned as one of the “Five Jewels”—*pancharatnâni* <note>original Sanskrit: "pancharatnâni" literally means "five jewels," referring to five particularly sacred sections of the Mahabharata.</note>—of Devanâgiri <term>Devanagari</term> <note>The "divine city" script used for writing Sanskrit and several modern Indian languages.</note> literature. In plain but noble language it unfolds a philosophical system which remains to this day the prevailing Brahmanic belief, blending as it does the doctrines of Kapila <note>The sage traditionally regarded as the founder of the Samkhya school of philosophy, which emphasizes dualism between spirit and matter.</note>, Patanjali <note>The author of the Yoga Sutras, whose system focuses on physical and mental discipline.</note>, and the Vedas <term>Vedas</term> <note>The oldest and most authoritative scriptures of Hinduism, containing hymns, philosophy, and ritual instructions.</note>. So lofty are many of its declarations, so sublime its aspirations, so pure and tender its piety, that Schlegel <note>August Wilhelm von Schlegel (1767–1845), a German scholar who produced the first Latin translation of the Bhagavad Gita and was a pioneer in Indology.</note>, after his study

<summary>This preface introduces a celebrated Sanskrit poem (the Bhagavad Gita) found within the Mahabharata, highlighting its status as a "jewel" of Indian literature and its synthesis of major philosophical schools.</summary>
<keywords>Mahabharata, Bhishma Parva, Sanskrit literature, Indian philosophy, Kapila, Patanjali, Vedas, Schlegel, Brahmanism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface's introduction to the Bhagavad Gita, moving from a description of its philosophical components to the profound emotional and scholarly impact it had on early European Indologists like Schlegel and Lassen.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

of the poem, breaks forth into this outburst of delight and praise towards its unknown author:

> “Reverence for teachers is counted by the Brahmins <term>Brahmins</term> <note>The priestly and scholarly varna (caste) in Hindu society, traditionally responsible for teaching and maintaining sacred knowledge.</note> among the most sacred duties of piety. Therefore, you first, most holy Bard and prophet of the Divine! Whoever you may have been called among mortals, as the author of this song—by whose oracles the mind is carried away to all things lofty, eternal, and divine, with a certain unspeakable delight—you first, I say, I bid hail, and I ever adore your footsteps.” <note>original Latin: “Magistrorum reverentia a Brachmanis inter sanctissima pietatis officia refertur. Ergo te primum, Vates sanctissime, Numinisque hypopheta ! quisquis tandem inter mortales dictus tu fueris, carminis hujus auctor, cujus oraculis mens ad excelsa quæque, æterna atque divina, cum inenarrabili quâdam delectatione rapitur— te primum, inquam, salvere jubeo, et vestigia tua semper adoro.”</note>

Lassen <note>Christian Lassen (1800–1876), a leading German orientalist of his time who contributed significantly to the study of ancient Indian languages and history.</note> re-echoes this splendid tribute; and indeed, so striking are some of the moralities here inculcated, and so close the parallelism — ofttimes actually verbal — between its teachings and those of the New Testament, that a controversy has arisen between Pandits <term>Pandit</term> <note>A Sanskrit scholar or learned master of Hindu philosophy and law.</note> and Missionaries on the point whether the author borrowed from Christian sources, or the Evangelists and Apostles from him.

This raises the question of its date, which cannot be positively settled. It must have been inlaid into the ancient epic at a period later than that of the

<summary>The preface highlights the intense admiration early European scholars held for the Bhagavad Gita's author and notes the historical debate regarding similarities between the poem's ethics and Christian teachings.</summary>

# PREFACE.

original *Mahábhárata* <term>Mahábhárata</term> <note>The "Great Tale of the Bharata Dynasty," one of the two foundational Sanskrit epics of ancient India.</note>, but Mr. Kaśinath Telang <note>Kashinath Trimbak Telang (1850–1893), a prominent Indian judge and orientalist scholar known for his English translation of the Bhagavad Gita.</note> has offered some fair arguments to prove it anterior to the Christian era. The weight of evidence, however, tends to place its composition at about the third century after Christ; and perhaps there are really echoes in this Brahmanic <term>Brahmanic</term> <note>Relating to the orthodox social and religious system led by the Brahmins, the priestly and scholarly class.</note> poem of the lessons of Galilee <note>A reference to the teachings of Jesus Christ; nineteenth-century scholars often debated whether the Bhagavad Gita was influenced by the New Testament.</note>, and of the Syrian incarnation.

Its scene is the level country between the Jumna <note>The Yamuna River, a major tributary of the Ganges.</note> and the Sarsooti <note>The Saraswati River, an ancient river of great spiritual significance in Vedic tradition, now largely dried up or subterranean.</note> rivers—now Kurnul and Jheend <note>Modern-day Karnal and Jind in the state of Haryana, India.</note>. Its simple plot consists of a dialogue held by Prince Arjuna <term>Arjuna</term> <note>One of the five Pandava brothers and a legendary archer; he represents the human soul facing a moral crisis.</note>, the brother of King Yudhisthira <note>The eldest Pandava brother, known for his righteousness and adherence to truth.</note>, with Krishna <term>Krishna</term> <note>A major deity in Hinduism, acting here as the supreme godhead disguised as a humble charioteer to guide Arjuna.</note>, the Supreme Deity, wearing the disguise of a charioteer. A great battle is impending between the armies of the Kauravas <term>Kauravas</term> <note>The one hundred sons of Dhritarashtra, representing the antagonistic side of the family in the epic conflict.</note> and Pândavas <term>Pândavas</term> <note>The five sons of Pandu, the heroic brothers fighting to reclaim their rightful kingdom.</note>, and this conversation is maintained in a war-chariot drawn up between the opposing hosts.

The poem has been turned into French by Burnouf <note>Eugène Burnouf (1801–1852), a foundational figure in French Indology.</note>, into Latin by Lassen, into Italian by Stanislav Gatti, into Greek by Galanos, and into English by Mr. Thomson and Mr. Davies, the prose transcript of the

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating and geographical setting of the Bhagavad Gita, outlining its central plot as a dialogue between Prince Arjuna and Lord Krishna on the eve of a great battle.</summary>
<keywords>Mahabharata, Arjuna, Krishna, historical dating, Kurukshetra, Pandavas, Kauravas, Indology, translation history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's survey of existing English and European versions of the Bhagavad Gita, specifically comparing his poetic approach to the scholarly prose and rhythmic versions produced by contemporaries like Kashinath Telang.</meta>

# PREFACE.

the last-named being truly beyond praise for its fidelity and clearness. <note>This likely refers to the Latin translation by August Wilhelm von Schlegel or the French version by Eugène Burnouf, both highly regarded for their precision in the 19th century.</note> Mr. Telang <note>Kashinath Trimbak Telang (1850–1893), an Indian scholar and judge whose prose translation of the Gita was published in the "Sacred Books of the East" series.</note> has also published at Bombay a version in colloquial rhythm, eminently learned and intelligent, but not conveying the dignity or grace of the original. If I venture to offer a translation of the wonderful poem after so many superior scholars, it is in grateful recognition of the help derived from their labours, and because English literature would certainly be incomplete without possessing in popular form a poetical and philosophical work so dear to India.

There is little else to say which the "Song Celestial" <term>Song Celestial</term> <note>The title given by the author, Sir Edwin Arnold, to his poetic English translation of the Bhagavad Gita.</note> does not explain for itself. The Sanskrit original is written in the *Anushtubh* <term>Anushtubh</term> <note>The standard epic metre of Sanskrit poetry, consisting of four quarters of eight syllables each, known for its rhythmic simplicity.</note> metre, which cannot be successfully reproduced for Western ears. I have therefore cast it into our flexible blank verse, changing into lyrical measures where the text itself similarly breaks. For the most part, I believe the sense to be faithfully preserved in the following pages; but Schlegel <note>August Wilhelm von Schlegel (1767–1845), a German poet and Indologist who produced the first critical edition of the Bhagavad Gita with a Latin translation.</note> himself had to say:

> "In the more obscure passages, I do not [dare] to affirm that I have always correctly divined the poet's mind—" <note>Original Latin: "*In reconditioribus me semper poetæ mentem rectè divinasse affirmare non [audeam]*". This is a modest admission from Schlegel regarding the difficulty of interpreting the most complex philosophical sections of the Sanskrit text.</note>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the author justifies his new poetic translation of the Bhagavad Gita, arguing that while scholarly versions exist, a "popular" poetic form is needed for English readers.</summary>

<keywords>Bhagavad Gita, Song Celestial, Edwin Arnold, Kashinath Telang, August Wilhelm von Schlegel, Sanskrit translation, Anushtubh metre, blank verse, 19th-century orientalism</keywords>

# THE SONG CELESTIAL;

->OR,<-

## <term>THE SONG OF THE LORD<note>original: *Bhagavad-Gîtâ*; "Bhagavad" refers to the Divine or the Lord (Krishna), and "Gita" means song. It is a central text of Hindu philosophy.</note></term>.

---

### CHAPTER I.

**DHRITIRASHTRA <note>The blind king of the Kuru kingdom, whose internal conflict and sons' ambitions lead to the great war.</note>:**

Ranged thus for battle on the sacred plain—
On <term>the Field of the Kurus<note>original: *Kurukshetra*; a site of both historical and spiritual significance in India where the climactic battle of the Mahabharata epic takes place.</note></term>—say, Sanjaya! say
What did my people and the <term>sons of Pandu<note>original: *Pandavas*; the five brothers who are the protagonists of the epic and the cousins—and rivals—to Dhritirashtra's sons.</note></term> do?

**SANJAYA <note>The king's attendant and charioteer, who has been granted "divine vision" to see the events of the distant battlefield and narrate them to the blind king.</note>:**

When he beheld the host of the <term>sons of Pandu</term>,
King <note>original: *Raja*</note> Duryôdhana <note>The eldest son of Dhritirashtra and leader of the forces opposing the Pandavas.</note> drew near to <term>Drona<note>The master of arms and royal preceptor who taught the art of war to both sides of the family.</note></term>,
And spoke these words: “Ah, <term>Teacher<note>original: *Guru*</note></term>! see this vast line,
How great it is, of the <term>sons of Pandu<note>original: *Pandu fighting-men*</note></term> fighting-men,
Drawn up for battle by the son of Drupada <note>Referring to Dhrishtadyumna, the commander of the Pandava army and a former pupil of Drona.</note>,

<summary>This page opens the Bhagavad Gita with King Dhritirashtra asking his visionary narrator, Sanjaya, for an account of the battle beginning at Kurukshetra. It introduces the primary leaders and the tense atmosphere as King Duryôdhana surveys the opposing Pandava army.</summary>

With blare to wake the blood, rolling around

Blew the great <term>Conch<note>original: *śaṅkha*; a ritual trumpet made from a large seashell, used to signal the start of the war.</note></term>; and, at the noise of it,

# BOOK THE FIRST.

Thus, as <term>Arjuna<note>The third of the Pandava brothers and a master archer; he represents the human soul seeking guidance.</note></term> prayed, (O <term>Bharata<note>original: *Bhārata*; a title for King Dhritarashtra, identifying him as a descendant of the legendary King Bharata.</note>!)
Between the hosts that heavenly <term>Charioteer<note>Lord Krishna, who has agreed to serve as Arjuna's driver without taking up arms himself.</note></term>
Drove the bright car, reining its milk-white steeds
Where <term>Bhishma<note>The great-patriarch of the family and the most respected elder on the battlefield.</note></term> led, and <term>Drona<note>The military teacher who trained both the Pandavas and their cousins, the Kauravas, in the arts of war.</note></term>, and their Lords.
“ See ! ” spake he to Arjuna, “ where they stand,
Thy kindred of the <term>Kurus<note>Specifically referring here to the sons of Dhritarashtra and their allies who have usurped the kingdom.</note> : ” and the Prince
Marked on each hand the kinsmen of his house,
Grandsires and sires, uncles and brothers and sons,
Cousins and sons-in-law and nephews, mixed
With friends and honoured elders ; some this side,
Some that side ranged : and, seeing those opposed,
Such kith grown enemies—Arjuna’s heart
Melted with pity, while he uttered this :

### ARJUNA:

<term>Krishna<note>The eighth avatar of Vishnu and Arjuna's spiritual guide; the name literally means "The Dark One" or "The All-Attractive."</note></term> ! as I behold, come here to shed
Their common blood, yon concourse of our kin,
My members fail, my tongue dries in my mouth,
A shudder thrills my body, and my hair
Bristles with horror ; from my weak hand slips

<summary>Arjuna is driven to the center of the battlefield by Krishna, where he is overcome with grief upon realizing he must fight and kill his own relatives, teachers, and friends.</summary>

<term>Gandîv<note>original: *Gāṇḍīva*; the divine, indestructible bow of Arjuna, a gift from the gods that allowed him to fire arrows with supernatural speed and power.</note></term>, the goodly bow ; a fever burns
My skin to parching ; hardly may I stand ;
The life within me seems to swim and faint ;
Nothing do I foresee save woe and wail !
It is not good, O <term>Keshav<note>original: *Keśava*; a name for Krishna meaning "The One with Beautiful Hair" or "Slayer of the demon Keshi." Arjuna uses this intimate name to appeal to his friend.</note></term> ! nought of good

Thus sadly won ! *Aho !* <note>An exclamation of distress or wonder, similar to "Alas!"</note> what victory

Seeing that these stand here, ready to die,

The rule of all <term>Three Worlds<note>In Hindu cosmology, this refers to the entire universe: the heavens (*Svarga*), the earth (*Prithvi*), and the netherworld (*Patala*).</note></term> ; then, how much less

<summary>Overcome by despair, Arjuna describes his physical collapse and refuses to fight, arguing that no kingdom or worldly power is worth the slaughter of his own kinsmen.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Arjuna’s lamentation from the previous page. Having expressed his horror at the prospect of killing his teachers and kinsmen, Arjuna now articulates the broader social and spiritual consequences he fears will follow the destruction of their royal lineage.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>BOOK THE FIRST.</header>

To seize an earthly kingdom! Killing these
Must breed but anguish, <term>Krishna</term>! <note>original: "Kṛṣṇa." The divine teacher and charioteer. The name literally means "The Dark One" or "The All-Attractive."</note> If they be
Guilty, we shall grow guilty by their deaths;
Their sins will light on us, if we shall slay
Those sons of <term>Dhritirashtra</term>, <note>original: "Dhṛtarāṣṭra." The blind king and father of the Kaurava brothers, who are Arjuna's cousins and enemies in this battle.</note> and our kin;
What peace could come of that, O <term>Madhava</term>? <note>original: "Mādhava." An epithet for Krishna, often translated as "Lord of Knowledge" or "Descendant of Madhu."</note>
For if indeed, blinded by lust and wrath,
These cannot see, or will not see, the sin
Of kingly lines o’erthrown and kinsmen slain,
How should not we, who see, shun such a crime—
We who perceive the guilt and feel the shame—
O thou Delight of Men, <term>Janârdana</term>? <note>original: "Janārdana." A name for Krishna meaning "the maintainer of all living entities" or "he who moves the masses."</note>
By overthrow of houses perisheth
Their sweet continuous household <term>piety</term>, <note>original: "dharma." In this context, it refers to the sacred family traditions and moral duties passed down through generations.</note>
And—rites neglected, piety extinct—
Enters impiety upon that home;
Its women grow unwomaned, <note>In the social context of the time, this refers to the loss of protection and social standing for women when the men of the family are killed, leading to the breakdown of traditional family structures.</note> whence there spring
Mad passions, and the mingling-up of <term>castes</term>, <note>original: "varṇa." The ancient social order. Arjuna fears that war will lead to social chaos and the loss of distinct community identities and duties.</note>
Sending a Hell-ward road that family,
And whoso wrought its doom by wicked wrath.

<summary>Arjuna argues that the war is morally unjustifiable because the destruction of a family line erodes ancient traditions and leads to total social and spiritual collapse.</summary>
<keywords>Arjuna, Krishna, family ethics, social order, Dharma, consequences of war, Janardana</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first chapter of the dialogue. Having argued that war destroys the family's moral foundations, Arjuna now focuses on the spiritual fate of his ancestors. He concludes that even being killed while defenseless is preferable to participating in such a massacre, leading to his total emotional collapse on the battlefield.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>THE SONG CELESTIAL.</header>

Nay, and the souls of honoured ancestors
Fall from their place of peace, being bereft
Of <term>funeral-cakes</term> <note>original: "pinda." These are ritual offerings of rice balls made to deceased relatives to sustain them in the afterlife.</note> and the wan <term>death-water</term>.¹ <note>original: "tarpan." A ritual of pouring water to satiate the spirits of ancestors.</note>
So teach our holy hymns. Thus, if we slay
Kinsfolk and friends for love of earthly power,
Alas! <note>original: "Ahovat!" An exclamation of grief, wonder, or horror.</note> what an evil fault it were!
Better I deem it, if my kinsmen strike,
To face them weaponless, and bare my breast
To shaft and spear, than answer blow with blow.

So speaking, in the face of those two hosts,
Arjuna sank upon his chariot-seat,
And let fall bow and arrows, sick at heart.

## CHAPTER II.

**SANJAYA :**

To him, who was filled with such compassion and such grief,
With eyes tear-dimmed and despondent, in stern words
The Driver, <term>Madhusudan</term> <note>original: "Madhusudana." An epithet for Krishna meaning "Slayer of the demon Madhu," symbolizing his power to destroy the demons of doubt and ignorance.</note>, thus addressed:

**KRISHNA :**

How has this weakness taken hold of you? Whence springs
This inglorious trouble, shameful to the brave,
Barring the path of virtue? Nay, <term>Arjun</term>! <note>A common shortening of the name Arjuna.</note>
Forbid yourself this feebleness! It mars
Your warrior-name! Cast off this coward-fit!
Wake! Be yourself! Arise, <term>Scourge of your Foes</term>! <note>original: "Parantapa." A traditional Sanskrit honorific for a hero who "scorches" or distresses his enemies through his valor.</note>

**ARJUNA :**

How can I, in the battle, shoot with shafts—

<summary>As Arjuna sits paralyzed by grief, Krishna rebukes his despair as unmanly and dishonorable, urging him to rise and fulfill his duty as a warrior.</summary>
<keywords>Sanjaya, Madhusudan, Krishna, Arjuna, Parantapa, warrior code, Kurukshetra, Bhagavad Gita Chapter 2</keywords>

<header>THE SONG CELESTIAL.</header>

> ->On <term>Bhishma</term><note>original: "Bhīṣma." The great-patriarch of the Kuru family and Arjuna's grand-uncle, who raised him.</note>, or on <term>Drona</term><note>original: "Droṇa." The master of arms and archery teacher who trained Arjuna since childhood.</note>—O thou Chief!—<-
> ->Both worshipful, both honourable men ?<-
>
> ->Better to live on beggar’s bread<-
> ->With those we love alive,<-
> ->Than taste their blood in rich feasts spread,<-
> ->And guiltily survive !<-
>

> ->Victor or vanquished here,<-
> ->When those confront us angrily<-
> ->Whose death leaves living drear ?<-
>

> ->My thoughts—distracted—turn<-
> ->To Thee, the Guide I reverence most,<-
> ->That I may counsel learn :<-
>

> ->Burned into soul and sense,<-
> ->If I were earth’s unchallenged chief—<-
> ->A god—and these gone thence !<-

<page-num>11</page-num>

## BOOK THE SECOND.

**SANJAYA :**

So spake <term>Arjuna</term><note>The protagonist prince and master archer who finds himself unable to fight his own kinsmen.</note> to the Lord of Hearts <note>original: "Hṛṣīkeśa." A title for Krishna meaning "Lord of the Senses" or "he who guides the heart."</note>,
And sighing, " I will not fight ! " held silence then.
To whom, with tender smile, (O <term>Bharata</term> !<note>Addressing King Dhritarashtra as a descendant of the legendary King Bharata.</note>)
While the Prince wept despairing 'twixt those hosts,
<term>Krishna</term><note>The divine incarnation (Avatar) acting as Arjuna’s charioteer, who now begins his role as a spiritual teacher.</note> made answer in divinest verse :

**KRISHNA :**

Thou grievest where no grief should be ! thou
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;speak'st
Words lacking wisdom ! for the wise in heart
Mourn not for those that live, nor those that die.
Nor I, nor thou, nor any one of these,
Ever was not, nor ever will not be,
For ever and for ever afterwards.
All, that doth live, lives always ! To man's frame
As there come infancy and youth and age,
So come there raisings-up and layings-down
Of other and of other life-abodes <note>A poetic description of reincarnation; the "life-abodes" refer to the physical bodies the eternal soul inhabits.</note>,
Which the wise know, and fear not. This that irks—

<summary>Arjuna falls into a despondent silence, prompting Krishna to begin his philosophical teaching. Krishna explains that wise people do not mourn the dead because the soul is eternal and merely moves through different bodies like it moves through stages of life.</summary>
<keywords>Sanjaya, Arjuna, Krishna, immortality of the soul, reincarnation, Bhagavad Gita Chapter 2, mourning, wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Krishna’s discourse to Arjuna on the battlefield of Kurukshetra. Having established that the soul is eternal, Krishna now explains how one should perceive physical sensations and the illusory nature of death to overcome the fear of killing or being killed in battle.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>THE SONG CELESTIAL.</header>

Thy <term>sense-life</term><note>original: "mātrā-sparśāḥ." Literally "the contact of the senses" with the material world, which produces the feelings of pleasure and pain.</note>, thrilling to the elements—
Bringing thee heat and cold, sorrows and joys,
'Tis brief and <term>mutable</term><note>Subject to constant change.</note>! Bear with it, Prince!
As the wise bear. The <term>soul</term><note>original: "Atman." The inner, eternal self that remains unchanged through various births and deaths.</note> which is not moved,
The soul that with a strong and constant calm
Takes sorrow and takes joy indifferently,
Lives in the life undying! That which is
Can never cease to be; that which is not
Will not exist. To see this truth of both
Is theirs who part <term>essence</term> from <term>accident</term><note>In a philosophical sense, "essence" (Sat) is the enduring reality, while "accident" (Asat) refers to temporary, superficial qualities that change.</note>,
Substance from shadow. Indestructible,
Learn thou! the Life is, spreading life through all;
It cannot anywhere, by any means,
Be anywise diminished, stayed, or changed.
But for these fleeting frames which it informs
With spirit deathless, endless, infinite,
They perish. Let them perish, Prince! and fight!
He who shall say, “Lo! I have slain a man!”
He who shall think, “Lo! I am slain!” those both
Know naught! Life cannot slay. Life is not slain! <note>This famous passage clarifies that the "I" or the true Self is never the actor in the act of killing, nor the victim of it, because the spirit is beyond physical destruction.</note>

<summary>Krishna teaches Arjuna that physical sensations are temporary and should be endured with detachment. He argues that because the soul is eternal and indestructible, the concepts of killing or being killed are ultimately illusions.</summary>
<keywords>Atman, sense-perception, dualism, immortality, Krishna's teaching, reality vs. illusion, Arjuna, duty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Krishna’s revelation to the warrior Arjuna on the battlefield. Following the previous page’s assertion that the soul is indestructible, Krishna here employs the famous metaphor of changing clothes to explain the cycle of reincarnation (Samsara).</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
## BOOK THE SECOND.

Never the **spirit** <term>spirit</term><note>original: "Aja" (unborn) or "Atman." The eternal, innermost self that is distinct from the physical body and personality.</note> was born; the spirit shall cease to be
    never;
Never was time it was not; **End and Beginning are
    dreams!** <note>In Hindu philosophy, the linear concepts of "start" and "finish" are considered part of *Maya* (illusion); the soul exists outside of time.</note>
Birthless and deathless and changeless remaineth the
    spirit for ever;
Death hath not touched it at all, dead though the
    **house of it** <note>A poetic reference to the physical body.</note> seems!

Who knoweth it exhaustless, self-sustained,
Immortal, indestructible,—shall such
Say, “I have killed a man, or caused to kill ?” <note>Krishna argues that because the soul cannot be destroyed, the act of killing in a righteous war does not destroy the actual being, only their temporary form.</note>

Nay, but as when one layeth
    His worn-out **robes** away,
And, taking new ones, sayeth,
    “These will I wear to-day!”
So putteth by the spirit
    Lightly its **garb of flesh**, <term>garb of flesh</term><note>original: "śarīrāṇi" (bodies). This is one of the most famous passages in the Gita, explaining reincarnation as a simple transition of the soul from one physical form to another.</note>
And passeth to inherit
    A **residence** afresh.

<summary>Krishna describes the soul as eternal and changeless, using the metaphor of discarding old clothes for new ones to explain how the spirit moves from one body to another after death.</summary>

## CHAPTER III.

**ARJUNA:**

> Thou whom all mortals praise, <term>Janârdana</term>! <note>original: "Janārdana." A title for Krishna meaning "the one who stirs or moves the people" or "the maintainer of mankind."</note>
> If meditation be a nobler thing
> Than action, wherefore, then, great <term>Keśava</term>! <note>original: "Keśava." A name for Krishna meaning "the one with long, beautiful hair" or "the slayer of the demon Keshi."</note>
> Dost thou impel me to this dreadful fight?
> Now am I by thy doubtful speech disturbed!
> Tell me one thing, and tell me certainly;
> By what road shall I find the better end?

**KRISHNA:**

> I told thee, blameless Lord! there be two paths
> Shown to this world; two schools of wisdom. First
> The <term>Sânkhya’s</term>, <note>original: "Sāṃkhya." The path of knowledge or intellectual analysis that seeks to understand the distinction between the soul and the material world.</note> which doth save in way of works
> Prescribed¹ by reason; <note>The translator notes: "I feel convinced sânkhyânân and yoginân must be transposed here in sense." This refers to the idea that the "Sankhya" path usually emphasizes knowledge/reason, while the "Yoga" path emphasizes works/action.</note> next, the <term>Yôg</term>, <note>original: "Yoga." Specifically "Karma Yoga," the path of disciplined, selfless action and devotion.</note> which bids

<summary>Arjuna asks Krishna why he must participate in a violent war if intellectual meditation is superior to action. Krishna responds by explaining that there are two established spiritual paths in the world: the path of knowledge (Sankhya) and the path of action (Yoga).</summary>

Rests any actionless; his nature's law
Compels him, even unwilling, into act;
<note>This refers to the three <term>Gunas</term> (qualities of nature)—goodness, passion, and inertia—which inherently drive all physical and mental movement.</note>

<note>The "allotted task" refers to <term>Dharma</term>, the inherent duty or righteous path one must follow based on their nature and position in life.</note>

### BOOK THE THIRD ###

Be free from desire, and you shall successfully perform
Your heavenly purpose. The Lord of Creatures <note>original: "Prajâpati," a Vedic deity presiding over procreation and the protection of life.</note> spoke—
In the beginning, when all people were created,
And, along with mankind, the rite of sacrifice—"Do this!
Work! Sacrifice! Increase and multiply
Through sacrifice! This shall be your Wish-Fulfilling Cow <note>original: "Kamadûk," the miraculous cow who grants all desires.</note>,
Your 'Cow of Plenty,' giving back her milk
Of all abundance. Honor the gods through this;
The gods shall grant you grace. Those things you crave
The gods will grant to Labor, when it pays
Its dues in the altar-flame. But if one eats
The fruits of the earth, giving to kindly Heaven
No gift of work in return, that thief steals from his world."

Those who eat food after their sacrifice
Are cleared of fault, but those who prepare a feast
Entirely for themselves, eat sin and drink sin.
By food the living survive; food comes from rain,
And rain comes through holy sacrifice <note>In this context, <term>Sacrifice</term> (Yajna) refers to any selfless action performed as an offering to the divine or for the benefit of the world.</note>,
And sacrifice is performed through the dues of labor;

<summary>Krishna describes how the Creator established the cycle of sacrifice and work at the beginning of time, explaining that prosperity comes from a reciprocal relationship with the divine. He warns that those who consume the world's resources without offering their own labor in return are effectively thieves.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse from Chapter III of the Bhagavad Gita, where Krishna explains the cosmic cycle of action. Having previously established that sacrifice sustains the world, he now describes the consequences for those who ignore this duty and the path to spiritual perfection through selfless work.</meta>

->[Page 28]<-

Thus action originates from the Supreme Spirit <note>original: "Brahmâ"; in this context, it refers to the Absolute Reality or the sacred word of the Vedas</note>, who is the One, the Only, the All-pervading; he is present at all times in the act of sacrifice. He who abstains from helping the rolling wheels of this great world, instead indulging his idle senses, lives a lost life—shameful and useless.

Existing only for himself, self-absorbed, and serving only his own needs, such a person has no part in the cosmic order; he achieves nothing. No action, whether completed or left undone, has any merit for him; no hope of helping any living creature on earth depends on him. <note>The original translator, Edwin Arnold, noted his uncertainty regarding the literal accuracy of this specific verse's rendering.</note>

Therefore, gladly perform the tasks prescribed to you with a spirit of detachment. By performing one’s plain duty without selfish attachment, a person ascends to their highest bliss. It was through work alone that <term>Janaka</term> <note>A legendary philosopher-king of Videha who achieved spiritual liberation while fulfilling his royal duties.</note> and the ancient saints reached a state of blessedness! Moreover, you should embrace action for the sake of upholding your fellow man and the world. Whatever a wise person chooses to do...

<summary>Krishna explains that all action originates from the Divine and that those who live only for themselves fall outside the cosmic cycle. He urges Arjuna to perform his duty with detachment, citing King Janaka as an example of attaining enlightenment through selfless work.</summary>

### BOOK THE THIRD.

The unwise people take; what best men do
The multitude will follow. Look on me,
Thou Son of <term>Prithâ</term>! <note>original: "Pārtha"; a name for Arjuna, meaning "son of Prithā" (his mother Kunti’s birth name).</note> in the three wide worlds
I am not bound to any toil, no height
Awaits to scale, no gift remains to gain,
Yet I act here! and, if I acted not—
Earnest and watchful—those that look to me
For guidance, sinking back to sloth again
Because I slumbered, would decline from good,
And I should break earth's order and commit
Her offspring unto ruin, <term>Bharata</term>! <note>original: "Bhārata"; a title for Arjuna as a descendant of King Bharata, the legendary founder of the dynasty.</note>
Even as the unknowing toil, wedded to sense,
So let the enlightened toil, sense-freed, but set
To bring the world deliverance, and its bliss;
Not sowing in those simple, busy hearts
Seed of despair. Yea! let each play his part
In all he finds to do, with <term>unyoked soul</term>. <note>A soul that is "unyoked" or "unattached" is one that performs actions without being emotionally bound to the success or failure of the outcome.</note>
All things are everywhere by <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Prakriti"; the primal creative force or material nature of the universe.</note> wrought
In interaction of the <term>qualities</term>. <note>original: "Gunas"; the three fundamental forces of nature—Sattva (goodness), Rajas (passion), and Tamas (darkness/inertia)—which dictate human behavior.</note>
The fool, cheated by self, thinks, "This I did"

<vocab>Prithâ, Bharata, Nature, qualities, unyoked soul</vocab>

<summary>Krishna explains that even though he, as the Divine, has nothing to gain through work, he continues to act to set an example for humanity. He teaches that while all actions are actually driven by the forces of Nature (the Gunas), the ignorant person mistakenly believes they are the sole author of their deeds.</summary>

# THE SONG CELESTIAL.

# BOOK THE THIRD.

Every being acts according to its own nature,
following the path that fits it best;
even the wise man follows his natural leanings.
What is the use of struggling against nature?
The objects of the senses will naturally stir the senses
into feelings of like and dislike; yet the enlightened man
must not surrender to these, for they are his enemies.

Finally, it is better for a person to do
his own <term>prescribed duty</term> <note>original: "Dharma"; specifically "Sva-dharma," the unique path or duty that aligns with one's own nature and position in life.</note> even if he does it poorly,
than to take up the tasks of another, even if he performs them well.
To die while performing one's own duty brings no ill;
but he who seeks to follow another’s road shall always be lost.

### ARJUNA:

Yet tell me, Teacher! By what hidden force is a man
driven to do wrong, even against his own will,
as if some power were pushing him down that evil path?

### KRISHNA:

->It is <term>Desire</term>!<- <note>original: "Kāma"; the Sanskrit term for longing, craving, or worldly desire.</note>

It is Passion! Born of the <term>restless quality</term>, <note>original: "Rajo-guṇa" or "Darknesses" in this specific transcription. This refers to the active, passionate energy in nature that drives greed and attachment.</note>

<summary>Krishna explains that it is better to fail at one's own duty than to succeed at another's, and identifies insatiable desire as the force that drives people toward wrongdoing.</summary>

## CHAPTER IV.

KRISHNA:

> This deathless <term>Yoga</term> <note>The practice of spiritual discipline and "union" between the individual self and the divine.</note>, this deep union,
> I taught Vivaswata,¹ the Lord of Light;
> Vivaswata to Manu <note>The primordial man and lawgiver of the current age.</note> gave it; he
> To Ikshwâku <note>The first king of the Solar Dynasty and an ancestor of many great heroes.</note>; so passed it down the line
> Of all my royal <term>Rishis</term> <note>original: "Rajarshis"; these are "royal sages," kings who possessed the deep spiritual insight of forest-dwelling seers.</note>. Then, with years,
> The truth grew dim and perished, noble Prince!
> Now once again to thee it is declared—
> This ancient lore, this mystery supreme—
> Seeing I find thee votary and friend.

ARJUNA:

> Thy birth, dear Lord, was in these later days,
> And bright Vivaswata's preceded time! <note>Arjuna expresses confusion here because Krishna appears to him as a contemporary man, yet Krishna claims to have taught the sun-god at the beginning of creation.</note>

---

<note>¹ A name of the sun.</note>

<summary>Krishna explains that the spiritual wisdom he is sharing is not new, but an ancient, eternal truth passed down through a lineage of sun-gods and royal sages. # BOOK THE FOURTH.

How shall I comprehend this thing thou sayest,
“From the beginning it was I who taught?”

### KRISHNA:

Manifold the renewals of my birth
Have been, Arjuna! and of thy births, too!
But mine I know, and thine thou knowest not,
O Slayer of thy Foes! <note>original: "Parantapa"; a common title for Arjuna, meaning "one who vexes or scorches his enemies."</note> Albeit I be
Unborn, undying, indestructible,
The Lord of all things living; not the less—
By <term>Maya</term> <note>The divine power or "magic" by which the absolute reality projects itself into the world of form and illusion.</note>, by my magic which I stamp
On floating Nature-forms, the primal vast—
I come, and go, and come. When Righteousness <note>In the original Sanskrit, this is "Dharma"—the moral order and cosmic law that sustains the universe.</note>
Declines, O <term>Bharata</term>! <note>A title for Arjuna as a descendant of the legendary Emperor Bharata.</note> when Wickedness
Is strong, I rise, from age to age, and take
Visible shape, and move a man with men,
Succouring the good, thrusting the evil back,
And setting Virtue on her seat again.
Who knows the truth touching my births on earth
And my divine work, when he quits the flesh

<summary>Krishna reveals the doctrine of the Avatar, explaining that he reincarnates throughout history to restore moral order (Dharma) whenever evil prevails. He distinguishes himself from Arjuna by noting that he remembers all his past lives while Arjuna does not.</summary>

# BOOK THE FOURTH.

Unchained by action, action binds not him;
And, so perceiving, all those saints of old
Worked, seeking for deliverance <note>The state of liberation from the cycle of birth and death, known as *Moksha*.</note>. Work thou
As, in the days gone by, thy fathers <note>Referring to the ancient ancestors and sages who performed their worldly duties as a form of worship.</note> did.

Thou sayst, perplexed, It hath been asked before
By singers and by sages, "What is act,
And what inaction?" I will teach thee this,
And, knowing, thou shalt learn which work doth save.
Needs must one rightly meditate those three—
Doing,—not doing,—and undoing <note>These three categories represent *Karma* (right action), *Akarma* (inaction/action without attachment), and *Vikarma* (forbidden or wrong action).</note>. Here
Thorny and dark the path is! He who sees
How action may be rest, rest action——he
Is wisest 'mid his kind; he hath the truth!
He doeth well, acting or resting. Freed
In all his works from prickings of desire,
Burned clean in act by the white fire of truth <note>original: "jnana-agni"; the "fire of wisdom" that consumes the karmic consequences of one's deeds.</note>,
The wise call that man wise; and such an one,
Renouncing fruit of deeds <note>The core practice of *Nishkama Karma*: acting because it is right, not because of what one might gain from it.</note>, always content,
Always self-satisfying, if he works,

<summary>Krishna defines the paradox of "action in inaction," explaining that a person who acts without personal desire or attachment to the results is spiritually free. He emphasizes that spiritual wisdom acts like a fire, purifying the individual of the karmic consequences of their deeds.</summary>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse in Chapter 4 of the Bhagavad Gita. Krishna is explaining to the warrior Arjuna that for a person who has attained spiritual wisdom, the very nature of "action" changes. He describes a state of total detachment where even physical actions do not create "karma" (spiritual debt) because the individual no longer sees themselves as the sole cause of their actions, but as part of a divine process.</meta>

## THE SONG CELESTIAL.

Doth nothing that shall stain his separate soul,
Which—quit of fear and hope—subduing self— <note>The "subduing of self" refers to *Atma-samyama*, the discipline of the mind and senses to prevent them from being led astray by worldly desires.</note>
Rejecting outward impulse—yielding up
To body's need nothing save body, dwells
Sinless amid all sin, with equal calm
Taking what may befall, by grief unmoved,
Unmoved by joy, unenvyingly; the same
In good and evil fortunes; nowise bound
By bond of deeds. <note>In Indian philosophy, every action usually leaves a "seed" or "bond" (karma) that requires a future result. The enlightened person, however, acts without personal desire, thus breaking this cycle of cause and effect.</note> Nay, but of such an one,
Whose crave is gone, whose soul is liberate,
Whose heart is set on truth—of such an one
What work he does is work of sacrifice,
Which passeth purely into ash and smoke
Consumed upon the altar! All's then God!
The sacrifice is Brahm, <note>original: "Brahman." In this context, it refers to the Supreme Reality or the Absolute that pervades the entire universe.</note> the ghee <note>original: "ghrita." Clarified butter, a staple offering in ancient Indian fire sacrifices.</note> and grain
Are Brahm, the fire is Brahm, the flesh it eats
Is Brahm, and unto Brahm attaineth he
Who, in such office, meditates on Brahm. <note>This is a pivotal verse (Chapter 4, Verse 24). It teaches that when a person is fully conscious of the Divine, the act of offering, the tool used, the fire, and the person offering are all seen as manifestations of the same universal spirit.</note>
Some votaries <note>Devoted followers or practitioners of religious rites.</note> there be who serve the gods
With flesh and altar-smoke; but other some

<summary>Krishna describes the spiritually liberated person as one who is free from desire and dualities like joy and grief. He explains that for such a person, every action becomes a holy sacrifice where the act, the offering, and the recipient are all recognized as the Divine (Brahman).</summary>

## THE SONG CELESTIAL.

Who, lighting subtler fires, <note>These "subtler fires" refer to internal spiritual practices rather than the physical fire used in traditional Vedic rituals.</note> make purer rite
With will of worship. Of the which be they
Who, in white flame of **continence**, <note>The term "continence" refers to *Brahmacharya*, the practice of self-restraint and the conservation of vital energy for spiritual pursuits.</note> consume
Joys of the sense, delights of eye and ear,
Foregoing tender speech and sound of song:
And they who, kindling fires with torch of **Truth**,
Burn on a hidden altar-stone the bliss
Of youth and love, renouncing happiness:
And they who lay for offering there their wealth,
Their **penance**, **meditation**, **piety**, <note>These refer to *Dravya-yajna* (sacrifice of material possessions) and *Tapo-yajna* (sacrifice through austerity or self-discipline).</note>
Their steadfast reading of the scrolls, <note>The "scrolls" refers to *Svadhyaya*, the disciplined study of sacred scriptures or the Vedas.</note> their lore
Painfully gained with long **austerities**:
And they who, making silent **sacrifice**,
Draw in their breath to feed the **flame of thought**,
And breathe it forth to waft the heart on high,

## CHAPTER V.

**ARJUNA:**

Yet, Krishna! at the one time you praise
The renunciation of works <note>Original: *sannyasa*. This refers to the traditional path of retiring from worldly duties to seek spiritual truth.</note>, and at another time,
Service through work <note>Original: *karma-yoga*. This is the practice of performing one's duties as a spiritual offering.</note>. Of these two, tell me plainly,
Which is the better way?

**KRISHNA:**

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To cease from works
Is well, and to do works in holiness
Is well; and both lead to bliss supreme <note>Original: *nihshreyasa*. This refers to the ultimate good or final liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</note>;
But of these two, the better way is his
Who performs his duties piously without withdrawing.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That is the true <term>Renouncer</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Sannyasi*. Traditionally, a monk who has given up everything. Here, Krishna redefines the term to mean anyone—monk or soldier—who has given up selfish desire.</note>, firm and fixed,

<summary>Arjuna asks Krishna to resolve the conflict between the path of monk-like renunciation and the path of active duty. Krishna responds that while both lead to liberation, performing one's duty with a holy mind is the superior path.</summary>

### CHAPTER VI.

**KRISHNA:**

Therefore, whoever does the work that is right to do,
Without seeking reward from that work, that man, O
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Prince!
Is both a <term>Sânyasi</term> <note>original: Sânyasi; a renunciant who has set aside worldly attachments</note> and a <term>Yôgi</term> <note>original: Yôgi; one who practices the discipline of spiritual union</note>—both in one.
And he is neither who does not light the ritual flame
Of sacrifice, nor sets his hand to his tasks. <note>In ancient India, householders performed daily fire rituals. Krishna is explaining that true spirituality is not about stopping these outward rituals, but about one's inner attitude.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Regard as a true Renouncer him who makes
Worship through his work; for no one becomes a
<term>Yôgin</term> <note>original: Yôgin; a practitioner of yoga</note> who does not renounce
selfish purpose. Thus it is well said:
“By works the <term>votary</term> <note>a devoted seeker or practitioner</note> rises to spiritual height, <note>The OCR reads "saith," but in this context, it refers to the "height" of spiritual attainment or the "state" of yoga.</note>
And <term>saintship</term> <note>a state of perfect serenity or spiritual mastery</note> is the ceasing from all self-driven works;”
Because the perfect <term>Yôgin</term> acts—but acts... <note>This sentence continues on the next page, explaining that the master of yoga acts without being attached to the objects of the senses or the results of those actions.</note>

<summary>Krishna begins Chapter VI by redefining renunciation, explaining that a true practitioner of yoga is not someone who simply abandons their duties or rituals, but someone who performs their work without seeking personal gain.</summary>

Unmoved by passions and unbound by deeds,

The Self by Soul, not trample down his Self,

    foe.
Soul is Self’s friend when Self doth rule o’er Self,
But Self turns enemy if Soul’s own self
Hates Self as not itself.¹ <note>original: Âtman; The original Sanskrit text relies on the double meaning of the word "Atman," which refers to both the individual "self" (the ego or mind) and the supreme "Self" (the eternal soul). ->The sovereign soul<-

Of him who lives self-governed and at peace
Is centred in itself, taking alike
Pleasure and pain; heat, cold; glory and shame.

With joy of light and truth; dwelling apart
Upon a peak, with senses subjugate <note>meaning the senses are brought under complete control</note>
Whereto the clod, the rock, the glistering gold
Show all as one. By this sign is he known

tranquil in spirit, free from fear, and firm
in his <term>Brahmacharya</term> vow <note>original: Brahmacharya; a vow of self-restraint, holy conduct, and often celibacy, intended to conserve energy for spiritual practice.</note>, devoted to Me.

That <term>Yôjin</term> <note>original: Yôjin; a practitioner of yoga who seeks union with the Divine</note>, so devoted, so controlled,
Comes to the peace beyond—My peace, the peace
Of high <term>Nirvana</term> <note>original: Nirvana; the ultimate state of spiritual liberation and the extinguishing of the fires of passion and ego</note>!

->But for earthly needs<-

Religion is not his who too much fasts
Or too much feasts, nor his who sleeps away
An idle mind; nor his who wears to waste
His strength in vigils <note>staying awake for long periods for religious purposes</note>. Nay, <term>Arjuna</term>! <note>The noble warrior and student to whom these teachings are addressed</note> call
That the true piety which most removes
Earth-aches and ills <note>referring to the suffering and instability of worldly life</note>, where one is moderate
In eating and in resting, and in sport;
Measured in wish and act; sleeping betimes,
Waking betimes for duty.

->When the man,<-

So living, centres on his soul the thought
Straitly restrained—untouched internally
By stress of sense—then is he <term>Yûkta</term> <note>original: Yûkta; meaning "yoked" or "integrated," describing a person who is perfectly disciplined and harmonized</note>. See!
Steadfast a lamp burns sheltered from the wind;
Such is the likeness of the <term>Yôgi's</term> <note>original: Yôgi; a person who has mastered the art of spiritual discipline</note> mind.

<summary>This page describes the "Middle Path" of yoga, explaining that spiritual success requires moderation in all physical habits—neither over-indulging nor extreme asceticism—to achieve a mind as steady as a sheltered flame.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, moderation, Middle Path, Arjuna, Yûkta, Yôgi, mental discipline, steadiness</keywords>

Shut from sense-storms <note>The "storms" of the senses refer to the turbulent and distracting nature of desires and external stimuli</note> and burning bright to
->Heaven.<-

Beyond all scope of sense, revealed to soul—
Only to soul! and, knowing, wavers not,
True to the farther Truth; when, holding this,
It deems no other treasure comparable,
But, harboured there, cannot be stirred or shook
By any gravest grief, call that state “peace,”
That happy severance <term>Yôga</term> <note>original: Yôga; here interpreted as the "un-yoking" or severance from suffering to achieve union with the divine</note>; call that man

->Steadfastly the will<-

And thought has passed from thinking. Shaking off
All longings bred by dreams of fame and gain,
Shutting the doorways of the senses close
With watchful ward; so, step by step, it comes
To gift of peace assured and heart assuaged,

<summary>This page defines the state of Yoga as a "happy severance" from pain and a realization of supreme joy found only in the soul. It emphasizes that this state is reached through a steadfast will that gradually quiets the senses and the ego.</summary>

### CHAPTER VII.

**KRISHNA:**

> Listen now, dear Prince! <note>Krishna is addressing the warrior Arjuna.</note> how, if your soul be set
> Ever on Me—still practicing <term>Yoga</term> <note>original: *Yôg*; the spiritual discipline of seeking union with the Divine.</note>,
> Still making Me your Refuge—you shall come
> Most surely unto a perfect grasp of Me.
> I will declare to you that utmost wisdom,
> In its entirety and in its specific details, which, when you know it,
> Leaves nothing more to know here in this world.

> Of many thousand mortals, one, perhaps,
> Strives for the Truth; and of those few that strive—
> Indeed, and rise high—only one—here and there—
> Knows Me, as I am, the very Truth.

> Earth, water, fire, air, ether, life-force, and mind, <note>These represent the elements of the physical and mental world that Krishna is about to explain as parts of his lower nature.</note>

<summary>Krishna begins a new discourse on the nature of divine knowledge, promising Arjuna a complete understanding of the Divine. He notes that while many people exist, very few truly seek or achieve a complete realization of the ultimate Truth.</summary>

### BOOK THE SEVENTH.

And individuality—those eight <note>This completes the list from the previous page: earth, water, fire, air, ether, mind, intellect, and individuality (the ego or "I-sense").</note>
Make up the showing of Me, Manifest.

These be my lower Nature; learn the higher,
Whereby, thou Valiant One! <note>Krishna is addressing the warrior prince Arjuna.</note> this Universe
Is, by its principle of life, produced;
Whereby the worlds of visible things are born
As from a <term>womb</term> <note>original: *Yoni*; the source, origin, or female reproductive organ, used here to describe the generative power of the Divine.</note>. Know! I am that womb:
I make and I unmake this Universe:
Than me there is no other Master, Prince!
No other Maker! All these hang on me
As hangs a row of pearls upon its string.
I am the fresh taste of the water; I
The silver of the moon, the gold o’ the sun,
The word of worship <note>This refers to the sacred syllable "Om."</note> in the <term>Vedas</term> <note>original: *Veds*; the oldest and most authoritative scriptures of Hinduism.</note>, the thrill
That passeth in the <term>ether</term> <note>original: *Akasha*; the most subtle of the five elements, often translated as space or the medium of sound.</note>, and the strength
Of man’s shed seed. I am the good sweet
->smell<-
Of the moistened earth, I am the fire’s red light,
The vital air moving in all which moves,

Whose mind is cheated by the show of things,

Four sorts of mortals know me: he who weeps,
Arjuna! and the man who yearns to know;
And he who toils to help; and he who sits
Certain of me, enlightened.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of these four,

# BOOK THE SEVENTH.

But I, Arjuna! know all things which were,
And all which are, and all which are to be,
Albeit not one among them knoweth Me!

By passion for the "pairs of opposites," <note>original: *dvandva*; these are the dualities of the material world, such as pleasure and pain, heat and cold, or success and failure, which distract the soul from the singular truth of the divine.</note>
By those twain snares of Like and Dislike, Prince!
All creatures live bewildered, save some few
Who, quit of sins, holy in act, informed,
Freed from the "opposites," and fixed in faith,
Cleave unto Me.

->Who cleave, who seek in Me<-
->Refuge from birth <note>The translator notes that he reads the Sanskrit word here as *janma* (birth), though some versions use *jara* (old age). In this context, it refers to the cycle of reincarnation.</note> and death, those have the<-
->Truth!<-

Those know Me <term>Brahma</term> <note>The Absolute, the Supreme Reality that is the source of everything.</note>; know Me Soul of Souls,
The <term>Adhyâtman</term> <note>The Supreme Spirit or the Essential Self that dwells within every individual.</note>; know <term>Karma</term> <note>The principle of action or the creative force that brings all beings into existence.</note>, my work;
Know I am <term>Adhibhûta</term> <note>The Lord of Beings; the divine essence as it underlies the perishable, material world.</note>, Lord of Life,
And <term>Adhidaiva</term> <note>The Lord of all the Gods; the divine essence as it underlies the spiritual or celestial realms.</note>, Lord of all the Gods,
And <term>Adhiyajna</term> <note>The Lord of Sacrifice; the divine presence that resides within every act of worship or self-offering.</note>, Lord of Sacrifice;

<summary>Krishna declares his total knowledge of time and existence, explaining that while most souls are lost in the dualities of attraction and repulsion, the truly faithful recognize him as the underlying essence of the self, action, and the cosmos.</summary>

### THE SONG OF GOD, <note>original: *Bhagavad-Gîtâ*</note>

->Entitled “The Yoga of Discernment,”<- <note>original: *Vijnânayôg*</note>

# BOOK THE SEVENTH.

But I, Arjuna! know all things which were,
And all which are, and all which are to be,
Albeit not one among them knoweth Me!

By passion for the "pairs of opposites," <note>original: *dvandva*; these are the dualities of the material world, such as pleasure and pain, heat and cold, or success and failure, which distract the soul from the singular truth of the divine.</note>
By those twin snares of Like and Dislike, Prince!
All creatures live bewildered, save some few
Who, quit of sins, holy in act, informed,
Freed from the "opposites," and fixed in faith,
Cleave unto Me.

->Who cleave, who seek in Me<-
->Refuge from birth <note>The translator notes that he reads the Sanskrit word here as *janma* (birth), though some versions use *jara* (old age). In this context, it refers to the cycle of reincarnation.</note> and death, those have the<-
->Truth!<-

Those know Me as the <term>Absolute</term> <note>original: *Brahma*; The Absolute, the Supreme Reality that is the source of everything.</note>; know Me as the <term>Supreme Spirit</term> <note>original: *Adhyâtman*; The Supreme Spirit or the Essential Self that dwells within every individual.</note>; know <term>Action</term> <note>original: *Karma*; The principle of action or the creative force that brings all beings into existence.</note>, my work;
Know I am the <term>Lord of Beings</term> <note>original: *Adhibhûta*; the divine essence as it underlies the perishable, material world.</note>, Lord of Life,
And the <term>Lord of all the Gods</term> <note>original: *Adhidaiva*; the divine essence as it underlies the spiritual or celestial realms.</note>, Lord of all the Gods,
And the <term>Lord of Sacrifice</term> <note>original: *Adhiyajna*; the divine presence that resides within every act of worship...</note>

<summary>This page concludes Chapter VII, where Krishna explains that while he knows all of time and existence, humans are often blinded by dualities like pleasure and pain. He identifies himself as the ultimate source of the Absolute, the Soul, and all creative action.</summary>

## CHAPTER VIII.

**ARJUNA:**

Who is that <term>Brahma</term>? <note>original: *Brahman*; The Absolute, the ultimate and unchanging reality that underlies all existence.</note>  What that Soul of Souls,
The <term>Adhyâtman</term>? <note>The Supreme Spirit or the Essential Self that dwells within every individual.</note>  What, Thou Best of All!

Thou namest <term>Adhibhûta</term>? <note>The Lord of Beings; the principle of the physical, perishable world.</note>  What again
Means <term>Adhidaiva</term>? <note>The Lord of Deities; the divine consciousness or the celestial agent behind the powers of nature.</note>  Yea, and how it comes

Slayer of <term>Madhu</term>! <note>An epithet for Krishna, referring to his defeat of the demon Madhu, representing the destruction of the ego.</note>  Further, make me know
How good men find thee in the hour of death?

**KRISHNA:**

I <term>Brahma</term> am! the One Eternal GOD,
And <term>Adhyâtman</term> is My Being’s name,
The Soul of Souls!  What goeth forth from Me,
Causing all life to live, is <term>Karma</term> called:

<note>This vocabulary list defines the primary actors and concepts discussed in this dialogue.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.

**KRISHNA:**

Now will I open unto thee—whose heart
Rejects not—that last lore, deepest-concealed,
That farthest secret of My Heavens and Earths,
Which but to know shall set thee free from

With inmost splendour shining; plain to see;
Easy to walk by, inexhaustible!

To grasp the greater wisdom, reach not Me,

<summary>Krishna explains that seekers who follow Vedic rituals only for heavenly rewards must eventually return to the cycle of rebirth once their merit is exhausted. However, those who worship him with singular devotion achieve eternal bliss, and even those who worship other gods are unknowingly offering their prayers to him.</summary>

<keywords>Indra, reincarnation, Vedas, Arjuna, Bhakti, karma, heaven, divinity</keywords>

Yet how shouldst thou receive, O Prince! <note>Arjuna, the warrior-prince and student of Krishna.</note> the vastness of this word?

I, who am all, and made it all, abide its separate Lord! <note>original: "ekamshena sthito jagat." This refers to the concept that the entire universe is supported by only a "single fraction" of the Divine. Krishna is teaching that while he is in everything, he is also much greater than the sum of all parts.</note>

## CHAPTER XI.

**ARJUNA:**

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This, for my soul’s peace, have I heard from
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Thee,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Named **Adhyâtman** <note>original: "Adhyâtman." This refers to the knowledge of the Supreme Self or the nature of the indwelling spirit.</note> ; comprehending which,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My darkness is dispelled ; for now I know—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O **Lotus-eyed** ! <note>original: "Kamalapatrâksha." A poetic title for Krishna, comparing the grace and shape of his eyes to the petals of a lotus blossom.</note>—whence is the birth of men,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of Thine immortal rule.  Fain would I see,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As thou Thyself declar’st it, **Sovereign Lord** !
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The likeness of that glory of Thy Form
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Wholly revealed.  O Thou **Divinest One** !
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If this can be, if I may bear the sight,

## CHAPTER XII. <note>This chapter is traditionally known as *Bhakti Yoga*, or the "Yoga of Devotion." It focuses on the relationship between the seeker and the Divine.</note>

ARJUNA: <note>The prince and disciple who is seeking spiritual guidance on the battlefield.</note>

->Lord! of the men who serve Thee—true in heart—<-
->As God revealed; and of the men who serve,<-
->Worshipping Thee Unrevealed, Unbodied, Far,<- <note>Arjuna is asking for a comparison between worshipping God in a personal form (like Krishna) versus worshipping an abstract, formless, and infinite Power.</note>
->Which take the better way of faith and life?<-

KRISHNA: <note>The Divine teacher, an avatar of Vishnu, who serves as Arjuna’s charioteer.</note>

->Whoever serve Me—as I show Myself—<- <note>This refers to *Saguna Brahman*, or God with attributes and form, which is often more accessible to human emotion and understanding.</note>
->Constantly true, in full devotion fixed,<-
->Those hold I very holy. But who serve—<-
->Worshipping Me The One, The Invisible,<-
->The Unrevealed, Unnamed, Unthinkable,<- <note>original: "Avyakta" — meaning the Unmanifest or the Indescribable essence of reality that cannot be perceived by the physical senses.</note>
->Uttermost, All-pervading, Highest, Sure—<-
->Who thus adore Me, mastering their sense,<- <note>The "mastering of sense" refers to the discipline of *Pratyahara*, where a practitioner withdraws their attention from worldly distractions to focus entirely on the inner Spirit.</note>

<summary>Arjuna asks Krishna whether it is better to worship God as a personal being or as an abstract, formless essence. Krishna begins his reply by praising those with steadfast devotion to His visible form.</summary>

Of one set mind to all, glad in all good, <note>This refers to equanimity, or *Samatvam*, the ability to see the Divine in every creature and remain undisturbed by the world's dualities.</note>
These blessed souls come unto Me.
Yet, hard
The travail is for such as bend their minds
To reach th’ **Unmanifest**. <term>Unmanifest: original "Avyakta." This refers to the infinite, formless aspect of God that has no physical attributes and cannot be perceived by the senses.</term> That viewless path
Shall scarce be trod by man bearing the flesh! <note>The teacher explains that for human beings, who are defined by their physical bodies and senses, it is psychologically and spiritually difficult to focus on a God that has no form or location.</note>

Musing on Me—him will I swiftly lift

the <term>Kshetrajna</term> <note>original: "Kshetrajna," literally "the Knower of the Field." This refers to the conscious spirit or witness that inhabits the body.</note>. In every "field" throughout the world, O Indian prince!

To me, true knowledge is that which understands both the known field

### KEY VOCABULARY
*   **Soothfastness**: The quality of purity and goodness (Sattva).
*   **Ignorance**: The quality of darkness and lethargy (Tamas).
*   **Passion**: The quality of energy and craving (Rajas).
*   **Qualities**: The three fundamental forces of nature (Gunas).
*   **Soul**: The eternal, individual spirit.
*   **Unlighted womb**: A rebirth into a state of low consciousness or animal life.
*   **Witlessness**: Total lack of spiritual awareness or discernment.

<summary>Krishna explains the karmic results of the three Qualities: Goodness leads upward to wisdom, Passion leads to restless desire and rebirth among the active, and Ignorance leads downward to darkness. Liberation is achieved by realizing that the Soul is separate from these acting forces.</summary>
<keywords>Gunas, Sattva, Rajas, Tamas, rebirth, enlightenment, Krishna, karma, three qualities</keywords>

## CHAPTER XV.

**KRISHNA :**

Men call the <term>Sacred Fig Tree</term> <note>original: "Aśwattha." In Hindu cosmology, this tree (Ficus religiosa) represents the entire manifested universe. It is described as "inverted" because its source is the Divine (the roots above) and its effects are the material world (the branches below).</note>,—the Banyan-tree,—
Which hath its boughs beneath, its roots above,—
The ever-holy tree. Yea! for its leaves
Are green and waving hymns which whisper
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Truth!
Who knows the <term>Sacred Fig Tree</term>, knows the <term>Vedas</term> <note>original: "Veds." The oldest and most authoritative scriptures of Hinduism.</note>, and all.

Its branches shoot to heaven and sink to earth, <note>Translator's note: I do not consider the Sanskrit verses here—which are somewhat freely rendered—"an attack on the authority of the Vedas," as the scholar Mr. Davies suggests, but rather a beautiful lyrical episode, a new "Parable of the fig-tree."</note>
Even as the deeds of men, which take their birth
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From <term>qualities</term> <note>original: "gunas." The three fundamental forces of nature: goodness, passion, and ignorance, which were the focus of the previous chapter.</note> : its silver sprays and blooms,
And all the eager verdure of its girth,

<summary>Krishna introduces the allegory of the Aśwattha, or sacred fig tree, which symbolizes the universe with its roots in the divine and its branches in the material world. He explains that understanding this tree is the key to understanding all sacred knowledge.</summary>

Sustaining them. And—dwelling thus beyond

Am called of men and <term>Vedas</term> <note>original: "Veda." The ancient and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism, composed in Sanskrit.</note>, Life Supreme,
The <term>PURUSHOTTAMA</term> <note>original: "Purushottama." Translated as "Supreme Person" or "Highest Soul." This represents the ultimate reality that transcends both the material world and the individual soul.</note>.

->Who knows Me thus,<-
->With mind unclouded, knoweth all, dear Prince!<-
->And with his whole soul ever worshippeth Me.<-

->Now is the sacred, secret Mystery<-
->Declared to thee! Who comprehendeth this<-
->Hath wisdom! He is quit of works in bliss!<-

## CHAPTER XVI.

**KRISHNA:** <term>Krishna</term> <note>The speaker is the eighth avatar of Lord Vishnu, acting as the divine guide and teacher.</note>

> Fearlessness, singleness of soul <note>original: "Sattva-samshuddhi." This refers to a state of mental purity and harmony.</note>, the will
> Always to strive for wisdom <note>original: "Jnana-yoga." The path of knowledge and intellectual discernment.</note>; opened hand
> And governed appetites; and piety,
> And love of lonely study; humbleness,
> Uprightness, heed to injure nought which lives <note>original: "Ahimsa." The practice of non-violence toward all living beings.</note>,
> Truthfulness, slowness unto wrath, a mind
> That lightly letteth go what others prize;
> And equanimity, and charity
> Which spieth no man’s faults; and tenderness
> Towards all that suffer; a contented heart,
> Fluttered by no desires; a bearing mild,
> Modest, and grave, with manhood nobly mixed,
> With patience, fortitude, and purity;

**Vocabulary and Concepts:**
*   **Krishna**: The Supreme Lord and teacher of the Gita.
*   **Wisdom**: The spiritual insight gained through discipline and meditation.
*   **Piety**: Devotion to sacred duties and religious observation.
*   **Humbleness**: Freedom from arrogance and the ego.
*   **Truthfulness**: Veracity in thought, word, and deed.
*   **Equanimity**: Maintaining a calm, even mind in both success and failure.
*   **Charity**: Selfless giving and kindness without judgment.
*   **Tenderness**: Compassion and mercy toward all sentient beings.
*   **Fortitude**: Spiritual and mental strength in the face of adversity.
*   **Purity**: Internal and external cleanliness and integrity.

<summary>This page begins the sixteenth chapter of the Bhagavad Gita, where Krishna lists the twenty-six "divine qualities" or virtues that lead to spiritual liberation. These traits, ranging from fearlessness and non-violence to self-control and modesty, form the ethical foundation for a seeker of truth.</summary>
<keywords>Krishna, Virtue, Ethics, Ahimsa, Sattva, Wisdom, Equanimity, Bhagavad Gita Chapter 16, Divine Qualities</keywords>

An unrevengeful spirit, never given
To rate itself too high;—such be the signs,
O Indian Prince! <note>The speaker, Krishna, is addressing Arjuna, the protagonist and warrior prince of the Pandava family.</note> of him whose feet are set
On that fair path which leads to heavenly birth!

Deceitfulness, and arrogance, and pride,

These be the signs, My Prince! of him whose
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;birth
Is fated for the regions of the vile. <note>original: "Asuras." In Hindu philosophy, these are beings characterized by materialism, ego, and power-seeking, often translated as "demons" or "un-godly ones."</note>

The Heavenly Birth brings to deliverance <note>The liberation from the cycle of rebirth, known as Moksha.</note>,

Brings into bondage. Be thou joyous, Prince!

Two stamps there are marked on all living men,
Divine and Undivine; I spake to thee

---

<margin>Of the <term>Asuras</term>, literally.</margin>

<sig>K</sig>

**Vocabulary and Concepts:**
*   **Asuras**: <note>original: "Asuras."</note> Spirits or beings who prioritize the material over the spiritual, often depicted as being in conflict with the gods.
*   **Heavenly birth**: <note>original: "Daivi sampad."</note> A destiny or character shaped by divine, virtuous qualities.
*   **Indian Prince**: Arjuna, the recipient of the Gita’s teachings.
*   **Deliverance**: Liberation from the suffering and cycles of the physical world.
*   **Bondage**: The state of being trapped in the cycle of birth and death (Samsara) due to ego and desire.
*   **Divine and Undivine**: The dual nature of human character, often categorized as *daivic* (godly) and *asuric* (demonic).

<summary>Krishna distinguishes between the virtues of a divine nature, which lead to spiritual liberation, and the vices of a demonic nature, which lead to further worldly entrapment. He reassures Arjuna that he is born with a divine destiny.</summary>

Has no Law, nor Order, nor a Lord,"
So they say: "nor has it risen by Cause
Following on Cause, in perfect purpose, <note>This refers to the denial of <term>Karma</term> (the law of moral cause and effect) and the denial of a divine creator or governing principle.</note>

Of little wit, dark-minded—give themselves
To evil deeds, the curses of their kind.
Surrendered to desires insatiable,
Full of deceitfulness, folly, and pride,
In blindness clinging to their errors, caught
In a sinful course, they trust this lie
As if it were true—this lie which leads to death—

Caught in a net of dark delusion and lost in their own desires, they sink down to the foul depths of <term>Naraka</term> <note>original: "Naraka." This refers to the various hellish realms or states of existence in Hindu cosmology where the soul undergoes purification through suffering after a life of unrighteousness.</note>. Being conceited, stubborn, and arrogant—and completely intoxicated by the wine of their own wealth—they perform religious sacrifices in name only. These offerings are merely for show, lacking the sincerity of ancient tradition and true faith. 

Devoted only to their own ego, to brute force, insolence, lust, and anger, these people are My enemies. They hate the Divine presence within their own bodies and within the bodies of others. Because they are cruel, hateful, and the most degraded of men, I cast them down again and again at the end of their lives into "devilish wombs" <note>original: "āsurīṣu yoniṣu." This signifies a rebirth into a state of existence characterized by extreme ignorance, violence, and spiritual blindness.</note>.

Birth after birth, being trapped in these demonic states and utterly deceived, they fail to reach Me, O sweet Prince Arjuna! Instead, they continue to tread the Nether Road <note>The downward spiritual path that leads further away from liberation and closer to total self-destruction.</note>.

There are three gates that lead to the soul's ruin and downfall:
The door of Lust, the door of Wrath, and the door—

So too is there a threefold way
