Due time, and to a meet recipient,
Is gift of *<term>Sattwan</term>*, fair and profitable.
<note>original: "Sattva." The quality of goodness and harmony. A "Sattvic" gift is one given as a matter of duty, to a worthy person who can offer nothing in return, at the right time and place.</note>

The gift selfishly given, where to receive
Is hoped again, or when some end is sought,
Or where the gift is proffered with a grudge,
This is of *<term>Rajas</term>*, stained with impulse, ill.
<note>original: "Rajas." The quality of passion and ego. Charity given with the expectation of a future favor or with a sense of regret is considered "Rajasic."</note>

The gift churlishly flung, at evil time,
In wrongful place, to base recipient,
Made in disdain or harsh unkindliness,
Is gift of *<term>Tamas</term>*, dark; it doth not bless! ¹
<note>original: "Tamas." The quality of ignorance and darkness. Giving without respect, at an inappropriate time, or to an unworthy cause is considered "Tamasic."</note>

->HERE ENDETH CHAPTER XVII. OF THE<-
->BHAGAVAD-GÎTÂ,<-
<note>original: "Bhagavad-Gītā." The Song of the Lord.</note>
->Entitled “*<term>Sraddhatrayavibhâgayôg</term>*,”<-
<note>original: "Śraddhātraya-Vibhāga-Yoga." The Yoga of the Division of the Threefold Faith.</note>
->Or “*The Book of Religion by the Threefold Kinds of Faith.*”<-

### CHAPTER XVIII.

ARJUNA:

Fain would I better know, Thou Glorious One!
The very truth—Heart's Lord!—of <term>Abstention</term>,
<note>original: "Sannyâs" or Sannyasa. In Hindu philosophy, this refers to the formal state of renunciation where one abandons worldly life and social ties to focus on the spiritual.</note>
And <term>Renunciation</term>, Lord!
<note>original: "Tyâga." While often used interchangeably with Sannyas, in the Gita, Tyaga specifically refers to the inward practice of "giving up" the ego and the desire for the results of one's work, even while continuing to perform duties.</note>
And what separates these two!

KRISHNA:

The poets rightly teach that <term>Abstention</term>
<note>original: "Sannyâs."</note>
Is the foregoing of all acts which spring
Out of desire; and their wisest say
<term>Renunciation</term> is renouncing fruit of acts.
<note>original: "Tyâga." Krishna defines this as performing one's duty without being attached to the success or failure of the outcome.</note>

There be among the saints some who have held
All action sinful, and to be renounced;
<note>This refers to certain ascetic schools of thought that believed any physical action creates "karma" (consequences) and therefore prevents the soul from being free. Krishna will go on to argue against this view.</note>

<summary>This page begins the final chapter of the Bhagavad Gita, where Arjuna asks Krishna to define the difference between formal renunciation and the inward relinquishment of the rewards of work. Krishna begins to explain that true renunciation is not necessarily stopping all activity, but rather acting without selfish attachment to the results.</summary>

<keywords>Arjuna, Krishna, Renunciation, Abstention, Sannyas, Tyaga, Karma, Desire, Bhagavad Gita, Hindu Philosophy</keywords>

As worship, penance, alms—must be performed ! ”
<note>These three—worship (Yajna), penance (Tapas), and alms (Dana)—represent the core ritual and ethical duties in the Vedic tradition.</note>

<note>This refers to "Nitya Karma," or the obligatory duties assigned to a person based on their nature and social role (Dharma).</note>
Never is meet ! So to abstain doth spring

<meta>This page continues Krishna’s discourse in Chapter Eighteen regarding the nature of action and renunciation. On the previous page, he began describing "Rajasic" renunciation—the act of abandoning duty simply because it is physically difficult or unpleasant.</meta>

->THE SONG CELESTIAL.<-

When one saith “ ’Tis unpleasing ! ” this is null !
Such an one acts from “ **passion** ; ” nought of gain
<note>original: "Rajas." In this context, "passion" refers to the quality of ego, desire, and restlessness. To give up a duty because it is uncomfortable is considered a lower, "Rajasic" form of renunciation that brings no spiritual merit.</note>
Wins his **Renunciation** ! But, <term>Arjun</term> !
<note>original: "Arjuna." The warrior prince to whom Krishna is speaking.</note>
Abstaining from attachment to the work,
Abstaining from rewardment in the work,
While yet one doeth it full faithfully,
Saying, “ ’Tis right to do ! ” that is “ **true** ” act

<note>This refers to "Sattvic" renunciation, where one performs their duty (Dharma) simply because it is right, without being motivated by the outcome.</note>
Unvexed if his work fail, if it succeed
Unflattered, in his own heart justified,
Quit of debates and doubts, his is “ **true** ” act :
For, being in the body, none may stand
Wholly aloof from act ; yet, who abstains
From profit of his acts is abstinent.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The fruit of labours, in the lives to come,
Is threefold for all men,—**Desirable**,
And **Undesirable**, and **mixed** of both ;
<note>original: "Ishta" (desirable/heavenly), "Anishta" (undesirable/hellish), and "Mishra" (mixed/earthly). These represent the types of rebirth or karma earned by those who are still attached to the results of their actions.</note>
But no fruit is at all where no work was.
<note>This suggests that for the true renunciant who has surrendered the ego's claim to action, no karmic "fruit" or debt is created to bind them to future rebirths.</note>

<summary>Krishna defines true renunciation as performing one's duty without attachment to success or failure. He explains that while the physical body cannot be entirely inactive, one who renounces the "fruit" (rewards) of their labor is free from the three types of karmic consequences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Eighteenth Book of the Bhagavad Gita. Krishna is shifting the discourse from the types of renunciation to the underlying mechanics of human action, explaining why the individual "Self" is not the sole cause of events.</meta>

Hear from me, Long-armed Lord! the makings
<note>original: "Mahabaho." A common epithet for Arjuna, signifying his strength and prowess as a warrior.</note>
->five<-
Which go to every act, in <term>Sânkhya</term> taught
<note>Sânkhya is one of the oldest schools of Indian philosophy. It focuses on categorizing the components of reality to achieve liberation through knowledge.</note>
As necessary. First the force; and then
<note>original: "Adhishthana." This refers to the "seat" of action, usually interpreted as the physical body or the field where action takes place.</note>
The agent; next, the various instruments;
<note>The "agent" is the ego or doer, and the "instruments" are the senses and organs of action.</note>
Fourth, the especial effort; fifth, the God.
<note>original: "Daiva." This refers to "Providence," "Destiny," or the divine influence. It represents the factors beyond human control—the cosmic laws or past karma—that determine an outcome.</note>
What work soever any mortal doth
Of body, mind, or speech, evil or good,
By these five doth he that. Which being thus,
Whoso, for lack of knowledge, seeth himself
As the sole actor, knoweth nought at all.
And seeth nought. Therefore, I say, if one—
Holding aloof from self—with unstained mind
Should slay all yonder host, being bid to slay,
He doth not slay; he is not bound thereby!
<note>This is a central, and often challenging, point of the Gita. Krishna argues that if a warrior acts without ego and in accordance with divine duty, the "sin" of killing does not cling to his soul because he understands he is merely an instrument of a larger cosmic process.</note>

Knowledge, the thing known, and the mind
->which knows,<-
These make the threefold starting-ground of act.
The act, the actor, and the instrument,
<note>Krishna here divides the components of action into two triads: the mental precursors (knowledge, the object, and the knower) and the practical execution (the deed, the doer, and the tools used).</note>

<vocab>Sânkhya: A philosophical system based on dualism; knowledge: Jnana; actor: Karta; instrument: Karana</vocab>

<summary>Krishna explains the five factors necessary for any action to occur—the body, the ego, the senses, the effort, and divine providence—concluding that the individual self is never the sole cause of an event. He introduces the tripartite components of knowledge and action that drive human existence.</summary>

For duty, not for love, nor hate, nor gain.
<note>This line completes the description of "true" or Sattvic action—work performed because it is right, without emotional attachment to the result.</note>
There is "vain" Action : that which men pursue

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;thing
Heedless of issues, heedless of the hurt
Or wrong for others, heedless if he harm

<note>Tamasic action is performed in ignorance or delusion, without regard for consequences, ethics, or one's own well-being.</note>

There is the "rightful" doer.  He who acts
Free from self-seeking, humble, resolute,
Steadfast, in good or evil hap the same,
Content to do aright—he "truly" acts.
<note>The "rightful" or Sattvic doer is characterized by emotional stability and a lack of ego, remaining balanced whether they succeed or fail.</note>
There is th' "impassioned" doer.  He that works
From impulse, seeking profit, rude and bold
To overcome, unchastened ; slave by turns
Of sorrow and of joy : of <term>Rajas</term> he !
<note>The Rajasic doer is intensely attached to the fruits of their labor, easily swayed by the "rollercoaster" of success and failure.</note>
And there be evil doers ; loose of heart,
<note>This begins the description of the Tamasic doer, who lacks discipline and focus.</note>

<summary>Krishna categorizes human actions and the people who perform them into three types based on their underlying nature: the selfless (Sattva), the ego-driven (Rajas), and the ignorant (Tamas).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Krishna's final discourse to Arjuna in the eighteenth chapter of the Bhagavad Gita. It shifts from the discussion of duty (Svadharma) to the specific qualities and internal state of one who is ready for final liberation and union with the Divine.</meta>

# BOOK THE EIGHTEENTH

->Devoted—with a heart grown pure, restrained<-
->In lordly self-control, foregoing wiles<-
->Of song and senses, freed from love and hate,<-
->Dwelling 'mid solitudes, in diet spare, <note>Referring to a frugal or simple diet intended to keep the mind calm for spiritual practice.</note><-
->With body, speech, and will tamed to obey,<-
->Ever to holy meditation vowed,<-
->From passions liberate, quit of the Self, <note>This refers to the "ego" or the small, limited sense of "I-ness" (Ahamkara) that creates a sense of separation from others.</note><-
<margin>✓</margin>->Of arrogance, impatience, anger, pride ;<-
->Freed from surroundings, quiet, lacking nought—<-
->Such an one grows to oneness with the <term>BRAHM<gloss>Brahman; the ultimate, infinite Reality, the Absolute, or the Ground of all Being</gloss></term> ;<-
->Such an one, growing one with BRAHM, serene,<-
->Sorrows no more, desires no more ; his soul,<-
->Equally loving all that lives, loves well<-
->Me, <note>Here, Krishna speaks as the Supreme Reality or Godhead.</note> Who have made them, and attains to Me.<-
->By this same love and worship doth he know<-
->Me as I am, how high and wonderful,<-
->And knowing, straightway enters into Me.<-
->And whatsoever deeds he doeth—fixed<-
->In Me, as in his refuge—he hath won<-
->For ever and for ever by My grace<-

***

**BRAHM** <note>original: "Brahman"</note>, **meditation**, **self-control**, **passions**, **devotion**, **grace**

<summary>This page outlines the ascetic and meditative discipline required for a seeker to achieve union with the Absolute (Brahman). It emphasizes that through devotion and the removal of ego, the individual recognizes the Divine in all things and attains ultimate liberation through grace.</summary>
<keywords>Brahman, meditation, ego-loss, Krishna, devotion, spiritual liberation, self-control, grace</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the final teachings of the eighteenth chapter, moving from the description of the disciplined ascetic to the final call for total surrender to the Divine. It connects the previous discussion on achieving oneness with Brahman to the practical necessity of action through faith.</meta>

# THE SONG CELESTIAL

->The Eternal Rest! <note>The final state of liberation or peace, known in Sanskrit as *Shanti* or *Moksha*.</note> So win thou! In thy thoughts<-
->Do all thou dost for Me! Renounce for Me!<-
->Sacrifice heart and mind and will to Me!<-
->Live in the faith of Me! In faith of Me<-
->All dangers thou shalt vanquish, by My grace;<-
->But, trusting to thyself and heeding not,<-
->Thou can'st but perish! If this day thou say'st,<-
->Relying on thyself, "I will not fight!"<-
->Vain will the purpose prove! thy qualities <note>Referring to the *Gunas*—the three fundamental forces or tendencies (harmony, passion, and inertia) that make up human nature.</note><-
->Would spur thee to the war. What thou dost shun,<-
->Misled by fair illusions, <note>In Sanskrit, *Maya*; the deceptive appearance of the world that leads the soul to forget its true nature.</note> thou wouldst seek<-
->Against thy will, when the task comes to thee<-
->Waking the promptings in thy nature set.<-
->There lives a Master <note>original: "Ishvara"</note> in the hearts of men<-
->Maketh their deeds, by subtle pulling-strings, <note>An allusion to the "Yantra-arudhani," describing living beings as if they are mounted on a machine or like puppets on a carousel moved by the Divine.</note><-
->Dance to what tune HE will. With all thy soul<-
->Trust Him, and take Him for thy succour, Prince!<-
->So—only so, Arjuna! <note>The warrior-prince to whom these teachings are addressed.</note>—shalt thou gain—<-
->By grace of Him—the uttermost repose,<-
->The Eternal Place!<-

***

**Eternal Rest**, **Renounce**, **Sacrifice**, **faith**, **grace**, **Arjuna**, **Master**, **repose**

<summary>This page contains Krishna’s final plea to Arjuna to surrender his ego and personal will to the Divine. It explains that one's internal nature and the "Master" within the heart ultimately direct all actions, making resistance to one's duty futile.</summary>
<keywords>Arjuna, Krishna, surrender, grace, Gunas (qualities), Ishvara (Master), Maya (illusion), duty, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Krishna and Arjuna in the eighteenth chapter. Having explained the paths of knowledge, work, and meditation, Krishna now offers his "last word"—the path of absolute devotion and surrender as the highest secret of all.</meta>

### BOOK THE EIGHTEENTH

->Thus has been opened to you<-
->This Truth of Truths, <note>In Sanskrit, *Guhyad guhyataram*—the most secret of all secrets, referring to the knowledge of the Divine.</note> the Mystery more hidden<-
->Than any secret mystery. Meditate!<-
->And—as you will—then act!<-

->Nay! but once more<-
->Take My last word, My utmost meaning have!<-
->Precious you are to Me; right well-beloved!<-
->Listen! I tell you for your comfort this.<-
->Give Me your heart! adore Me! serve Me! cling<-
->In faith and love and reverence to Me!<-
->So shall you come to Me! I promise true,<-
->For you are sweet to Me!<-

->And let go those—<-
->Rites and writ duties! <note>original: "Sarva-dharman." This refers to all social, religious, and ethical obligations. Krishna tells Arjuna that total surrender to God supersedes all other rules.</note> Fly to Me alone!<-
->Make Me your single refuge! <note>original: "Ekam sharanam." This verse is often called the *Charama Shloka* (Final Verse), considered the essence of the Gita's message: that divine grace is the ultimate path to liberation.</note> I will free<-
->Your soul from all its sins! Be of good cheer!<-

->[Hide, the holy Krishna says,<-
->This from him who has no faith,<-

<summary>Krishna delivers his final and most profound teaching, urging Arjuna to abandon all complex religious duties in favor of total surrender to the Divine. This "Truth of Truths" promises absolute liberation from sin through faith and love alone.</summary>
<keywords>Bhagavad Gita, Krishna, Arjuna, surrender, Dharma, grace, devotion, Sharanagati, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the concluding portion of the eighteenth chapter of the Bhagavad Gita. After delivering the "Final Verse" on the previous page, Krishna now explains the "Phalashruti"—the spiritual rewards and merits gained by those who share, study, or hear this sacred dialogue.</meta>

# THE SONG CELESTIAL

->Never speak of this truth to one who lacks self-discipline,<-
->To one who does not worship, or who has no desire to listen;<-
->And hide it from all those who mock the Divine.<-
->But for the one who teaches this most secret<-
->And divine wisdom to those who love Me—<-
->Acting with supreme devotion and offering<-
->All honor to Me—that person shall without doubt<-
->Come to the Absolute! <note>original: "Brahma." In this context, it refers to Brahman, the ultimate, infinite reality or the Divine Source, rather than the specific creator god.</note><-

->Indeed, you will not find among all humanity<-
->Anyone who performs a service more dear to Me than this;<-
->Nor shall anyone on this earth<-
->Be more beloved to Me than they.<-

->Furthermore, whoever studies this sacred conversation<-
->Held between us here upon the plain, <note>Referring to the battlefield of Kurukshetra, the "Plain of the Kurus," where the dialogue takes place. The "sacrifice of knowledge" mentioned here implies that intellectual study of the Gita is a form of worship.</note><-
->Pondering it with a faithful and eager heart,<-
->That person has offered Me the sacrifice of knowledge!<-
->(Thus speaks Krishna!)<-

<summary>Krishna describes the spiritual merits of the Bhagavad Gita, stating that those who teach its message to devotees or study its dialogue are performing the highest service and will achieve union with the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Bhagavad Gita, Krishna, Brahman, Kurukshetra, devotion, sacrifice of knowledge, spiritual teaching, Brahman</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between Krishna and Arjuna in the eighteenth chapter, "The Yoga of Liberation through Renunciation." Following the description of the merits of teaching the Gita, this section focuses on the spiritual rewards for the listener and Arjuna's final moment of enlightenment.</meta>

# BOOK THE EIGHTEENTH

->**And even the person who, filled with faith,**<-
->**Listens to this wisdom with a humble heart—**<-
->**When they pass from this life, their soul shall surely rise**<-
->**To those radiant realms where the righteous,** <note>original: "shubhan lokan." These are the auspicious celestial worlds or states of being earned through merit and good deeds.</note><-
->**Having been freed from the bonds of the flesh, dwell in joy.**<-

Have you heard these words, O Prince, with a focused and attentive mind? Has the confusion caused by your ignorance <note>original: "ajnana-sammohah." This refers to the spiritual blindness and emotional delusion that paralyzed Arjuna at the beginning of the battle.</note> finally disappeared, Arjuna?

**ARJUNA:**

My delusion is destroyed! Through Your grace, O Unchanging Lord, I have regained my wisdom. <term>Smritir: Literally "memory" or "recollection." In this context, it means Arjuna has remembered his true nature and his duty (Dharma) which he had forgotten in his grief.</term> I stand firm now, and my doubts have all vanished. I am ready to act according to Your word! <note>This marks the climax of the poem; Arjuna, who had dropped his bow in despair in the first chapter, now resolves to fulfill his role in the great struggle.</note>

---

<vocab>Arjuna: The warrior-prince; Indian Prince: A title for Arjuna; faith: <term>shraddha</term> or soulful trust; ignorance: the clouding of the mind; Light: the dawning of spiritual truth; Lord: Krishna as the Divine Chieftain.</vocab>

<summary>Arjuna declares that his confusion and doubts have been dispelled by Krishna's teachings, signaling his readiness to fulfill his duty. The text also promises that those who listen to this dialogue with faith will attain higher spiritual realms.</summary>
<keywords>Arjuna, Krishna, enlightenment, delusion, faith, dharma, spiritual liberation, memory regained</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the epilogue to the Bhagavad Gita. Having concluded the dialogue between Krishna and Arjuna, the focus shifts to Sanjaya, the visionary narrator, who expresses his personal awe and provides a final blessing to King Dhritarashtra.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>THE SONG CELESTIAL</header>

### SANJAYA:

->Thus gathered I the gracious speech of Krishna,<-
->O my King! <note>Sanjaya addresses the blind King Dhritarashtra, to whom he has been narrating the entire battle and dialogue through the gift of divine vision.</note><-
->Thus have I told, with heart a-thrill, this wise and<-
->wondrous thing<-
->By great Vyâsa’s <note>Vyâsa is the legendary sage and poet credited with composing the Mahabharata; it was he who granted Sanjaya the "divine eye" to see the events from afar.</note> learning writ, how Krishna’s self<-
->made known<-
->The <term>Yôga: The spiritual discipline of union with the Divine.</term>, being Yôga’s Lord. So is the high<-
->truth shown!<-
->And aye, when I remember, O Lord my King,<-
->again<-
->Arjuna and the God in talk, and all this holy<-
->strain,<-
->Great is my gladness: when I muse that splendour,<-
->passing speech,<-
->Of <term>Hari: A name for Krishna or Vishnu, literally meaning "the one who removes" (evil or sorrow).</term>, visible and plain, there is no tongue to<-
->reach<-
->My marvel and my love and bliss. O Archer-<-
->Prince! all hail!<-

<vocab>Sanjaya: the narrator; Krishna: the Divine teacher; Vyâsa: the sage-author; Yôga: spiritual union; Arjuna: the warrior-disciple; Hari: the Divine Lord; Archer-Prince: Arjuna.</vocab>

<summary>Sanjaya concludes his narration by expressing his profound joy and wonder at having witnessed the sacred conversation between Krishna and Arjuna, attributing his ability to do so to the sage Vyâsa.</summary>
<keywords>Sanjaya, Krishna, Vyasa, Yoga, Hari, Arjuna, divine vision, spiritual ecstasy</keywords>
